I haven’t been keeping track of it. I haven’t noticed feeling especially upbeat or overly confident. Sometimes my mind jumps around a bit, but I wouldn’t say my thoughts are racing or that I’m talking faster than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you take less pleasure in things or have lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I still enjoy the usual things when I get around to them. I haven’t noticed losing interest or feeling like things aren’t pleasurable anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel rested and ready to go even after only a few hours of sleep?\n\nPatient: No, I can’t say I’ve noticed that. If I only get a few hours of sleep, I usually don’t feel especially rested or ready to go.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you need significantly less sleep than usual, like feeling fully energized after only a few hours of sleep?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t noticed that. When I don’t sleep much, I usually feel tired, not like my body can just keep going.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel like you don’t need as much sleep as usual, even if you're still active during the day?\n\nPatient: No, not really. If I’m short on sleep, I notice it during the day. I don’t get that feeling like I can stay active and not need the rest.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t get that feeling like I can stay active and not need the rest.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you need significantly less sleep than usual, like feeling fully energized after only a few hours of sleep?\n\nPatient: No, I can’t say I’ve noticed that. If I only get a few hours of sleep, I don’t feel fully energized—I usually feel like I didn’t sleep enough.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel like you can't stop talking, or that your thoughts are moving so fast you can't keep up?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed that, no. I might talk a bit when I’m engaged in something, but not like I can’t stop, and I don’t feel like my thoughts are racing faster than I can handle.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period lasting at least two weeks where you felt down, depressed, or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I’ve had days where I felt down, sure, but not for two weeks straight most of the day like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel like you can’t stop talking once you start, or that your thoughts are jumping from one idea to another so quickly that it’s hard to focus?\n\nPatient: Not so much that I can’t stop talking, no. It’s more that my attention jumps. Like if I’m trying to paint or plan out a story and I hear a truck outside, or I suddenly think about something in the garden, my mind goes right over there and then I lose where I was.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
